I know you’ve asked it a few ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult. I just can’t quite tell. The days don’t feel divided up neatly anymore—morning, afternoon, evening, they sort of blur.

I know I haven’t felt like myself, but I don’t know how to measure it that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, your mood is low or depressed for the entire day, or do you have times during the day when you feel more like yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, doctor. “Entire day” sounds so… exact. There are parts of the day where I’m just quiet, or heavy, I suppose, and then I’ll get up to make tea and remember I left the sugar tin in the wrong cupboard—my husband used to tease me about that, years ago, because I’d put things in the strangest places when I was distracted.\n\nBut feeling more like myself… I’m not sure I’d recognize it right now. Maybe little moments, maybe not. I haven’t been watching it by the clock.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you’re awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down. I just… I don’t know that I can say “majority of the time.” Some mornings feel very dim, and then I’ll get distracted by some little thing—like whether I paid the water bill, or I’ll notice dust on the lampshade and think I really ought to clean that, though I don’t always do it.\n\nSo I suppose there’s low feelings, yes, but whether it’s most of the time I’m awake… I haven’t been keeping track like that. It all sort of runs together lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the time you're awake, even if it's not the whole day?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked it a few ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult. I just can’t quite tell. The days don’t feel divided up neatly anymore—morning, afternoon, evening, they sort of blur.\n\nI can say I’ve been low, yes, but “most of the time I’m awake”… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just sitting there staring at nothing, and then I’ll suddenly remember I meant to rinse out a cup or move the newspaper, and for a minute I’m thinking about that instead. So I don’t know how to count it, really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I came because I just don’t feel right inside. Even when my children come and sit with me, or the sun is shining on my garden — and I used to notice things like that, you know, the roses especially — I feel flat. Like the part of me that could feel comforted or glad has just shut off. It’s a strange thing to try to explain.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your children?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s it. I used to get such a feeling from my garden, especially when the light came across it in the morning. My children come and sit with me too, and I know I should feel comforted by that—well, I used to. Even when my children come and sit with me, or the sun is shining on my garden — and I used to notice things like that, you know, the roses especially — I feel flat. Like the part of me that could feel comforted or glad has just shut off. It’s a strange thing to try to explain.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your children?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s it. I used to get such a feeling from my garden, especially when the light came across it in the morning. My children come and sit with me too, and I know I should feel comforted by that—well, I used to. But now it’s like I’m watching it all from behind glass. I feel flat inside, like the part of me that could be glad has just… shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I don’t know. I just know I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a straight yes or no, but I keep losing hold of it. I’m not sure. I don’t know that I can say “most days, most of the day” exactly. It feels heavy a lot, but then time gets funny, you know? I’ll be sitting in the kitchen and notice the pattern on the tablecloth—my sister had one with little blue flowers, years ago—and then I realize I’ve lost track of whether I was feeling low right then or just… somewhere else.

It isn’t something I’ve measured.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, your low mood is present for a significant portion of the day, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean yes or no. “Significant portion” sounds… official, and I don’t know how to judge that from inside the day.\n\nThere are times I feel quite low, and then I’ll drift off thinking about something else—like whether I left the porch light on, or how my mother used to keep rubber bands in a coffee tin, which is a silly thing to remember in the middle of all this. So yes, it’s there, but whether it’s a significant portion on most days… I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most days, most of the day” exactly. It feels heavy a lot, but then time gets funny, you know? I’ll be sitting in the kitchen and notice the pattern on the tablecloth—my sister had one with little blue flowers, years ago—and then I realize I’ve lost track of whether I was feeling low right then or just… somewhere else.\n\nSo I’m not trying to avoid it. I just don’t know how to measure it that way. It’s there, but I can’t honestly say how much of the day.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you are awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yes… I think that’s part of why I came. Even when my children sit with me, or when the sun is out on the garden—things that ought to make me feel better—I just feel flat inside.

It’s strange, because I used to notice the light on the leaves, you know, the way it would catch on the tomato cages… my husband used to say I fussed over those plants like they were babies. But now it’s like the part of me that could feel glad, or comforted, has just shut off. I can be there with them, but I don’t really feel it the way I should.

What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I came because I just don’t feel right inside. Even when my children come and sit with me, or the sun is shining on my garden — and I used to notice things like that, you know, the roses especially — I feel flat. Like the part of me that could feel comforted or glad has just shut off. It’s a strange thing to try to explain.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your children?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s it. I used to get such a feeling from my garden, especially when the light came across it in the morning. My children come and sit with me too, and I know I should feel comforted by that—well, I used to. But now it’s like I’m watching it all from behind glass. I feel flat inside, like the part of me that could be glad has just… shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. Down, maybe, but then some days I’m just sort of sitting there and I can’t quite tell what I’m feeling. My daughter says I seem quieter, but she always was one to notice everything—when she was little she’d notice if I moved a vase an inch.\n\nSo… most of the day, nearly every day? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that neatly, dear. Some days I feel low, yes, or… sort of emptied out. But then I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table and start thinking about whether I watered the fern, or whether my son fixed that loose step like he said he would, and I realize I haven’t really noticed my mood in that way.\n\nSo… most of the day, most days? I don’t know. I just know I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a straight yes or no, but I keep losing hold of it. Some days I sit by the window and everything feels heavy, and then I notice the neighbor’s cat in the hydrangeas and I start thinking I ought to tell her, because last summer it flattened a whole patch of flowers.\n\nSo… low for most of the day, most days? I’m not sure. I know I haven’t felt like myself, but I don’t know how to measure it that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, your mood is low or depressed for the entire day, or do you have times during the day when you feel more like yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, doctor. “Entire day” sounds so… exact. There are parts of the day where I’m just quiet, or heavy, I suppose, and then I’ll get up to make tea and remember I left the sugar tin in the wrong cupboard—my husband used to tease me about that, years ago, because I’d put things in the strangest places when I was distracted.\n\nBut feeling more like myself… I’m not sure I’d recognize it right now. Maybe little moments, maybe not. I haven’t been watching it by the clock.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you’re awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down. I just… I don’t know that I can say “majority of the time.” Some mornings feel very dim, and then I’ll get distracted by some little thing—like whether I paid the water bill, or I’ll notice dust on the lampshade and think I really ought to clean that, though I don’t always do it.\n\nSo I suppose there’s low feelings, yes, but whether it’s most of the time I’m awake… I haven’t been keeping track like that. It all sort of runs together lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the time you're awake, even if it's not the whole day?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked it a few ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult. So I don’t know how to count it, really.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for a good part of the day, even if it's not all day long?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. A good part of the day, yes maybe, but then I think—what counts as a good part? It isn’t something I’ve measured.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, your low mood is present for a significant portion of the day, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean yes or no. “Significant portion” sounds… official, and I don’t know how to judge that from inside the day.\n\nThere are times I feel quite low, and then I’ll drift off thinking about something else—like whether I left the porch light on, or how my mother used to keep rubber bands in a coffee tin, which is a silly thing to remember in the middle of all this. Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: I remember him falling and the men working on him in the kitchen, but I cannot remember what I said on the phone or how the paramedics got through the door, no matter how hard I try.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: When I’m in the house I keep listening for every little sound, checking the hallway and the back door, and I don’t feel safe being in the kitchen by myself anymore.\n- key: S080\n  name: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: If the refrigerator clicks on or someone knocks, I jump so hard my whole body shakes, and it takes me a long while before my heart settles down.\n- key: S082\n  name: Depersonalization_Derealization\n  description: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.\n  manifestation: Sometimes I walk through my own kitchen and it looks like a room in someone else’s house, like I’m moving around in a dream and not really there.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have stopped going to the senior center and I cannot manage meals, errands, or my calendar without my daughter stepping in, so I mostly stay in the house and avoid the kitchen unless someone is with me.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 weeks\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two weeks, ever since my husband collapsed and the paramedics worked on him in our kitchen.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 81 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S076\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most days, most of the day” exactly. It feels heavy a lot, but then time gets funny, you know? I’ll be sitting in the kitchen and notice the pattern on the tablecloth—my sister had one with little blue flowers, years ago—and then I realize I’ve lost track of whether I was feeling low right then or just… somewhere else.\n\nSo I’m not trying to avoid it. I just don’t know how to measure it that way. It’s there, but I can’t honestly say how much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you are awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down, and I’m not doing a very good job. “Majority of the time I’m awake” is hard for me to say. Some parts of the day I feel very low, and then other parts I’m just sort of… distracted, or I’m watching the kettle, or thinking about where I put my glasses.\n\nI used to keep my glasses on a little chain, you know, my aunt had one with fake pearls on it—funny what comes back to you. But to answer you, I don’t really know if it’s the majority of the day. I haven’t been able to tell it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came. Even when my children sit with me, or when the sun is out on the garden—things that ought to make me feel better—I just feel flat inside.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to notice the light on the leaves, you know, the way it would catch on the tomato cages… my husband used to say I fussed over those plants like they were babies. But now it’s like the part of me that could feel glad, or comforted, has just shut off. I can be there with them, but I don’t really feel it the way I should.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m still not sure how to answer that cleanly. It’s… it comes over me, yes, but “most of the day” feels hard to judge.

Some mornings I’ll sit there with my tea and it feels very low, like everything has gone quiet inside. Then I get pulled into noticing some little thing—the crack in the mug, or I start thinking about the old kitchen curtains we had—and suddenly I don’t know how much time passed or what I was feeling.

So I don’t want to say yes if that means I’m certain. I just know it’s there a lot, but I can’t really measure it the way you’re asking.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I came because I just don’t feel right inside. Even when my children come and sit with me, or the sun is shining on my garden — and I used to notice things like that, you know, the roses especially — I feel flat. Like the part of me that could feel comforted or glad has just shut off. It’s a strange thing to try to explain.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your children?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s it. I used to get such a feeling from my garden, especially when the light came across it in the morning. My children come and sit with me too, and I know I should feel comforted by that—well, I used to. But now it’s like I’m watching it all from behind glass. I feel flat inside, like the part of me that could be glad has just… shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. Down, maybe, but then some days I’m just sort of sitting there and I can’t quite tell what I’m feeling. My daughter says I seem quieter, but she always was one to notice everything—when she was little she’d notice if I moved a vase an inch.\n\nSo… most of the day, nearly every day? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that neatly, dear. Some days I feel low, yes, or… sort of emptied out. But then I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table and start thinking about whether I watered the fern, or whether my son fixed that loose step like he said he would, and I realize I haven’t really noticed my mood in that way.\n\nSo… most of the day, most days? I don’t know. I just know I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a straight yes or no, but I keep losing hold of it. Some days I sit by the window and everything feels heavy, and then I notice the neighbor’s cat in the hydrangeas and I start thinking I ought to tell her, because last summer it flattened a whole patch of flowers.\n\nSo… low for most of the day, most days? I’m not sure. I know I haven’t felt like myself, but I don’t know how to measure it that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, your mood is low or depressed for the entire day, or do you have times during the day when you feel more like yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, doctor. “Entire day” sounds so… exact. There are parts of the day where I’m just quiet, or heavy, I suppose, and then I’ll get up to make tea and remember I left the sugar tin in the wrong cupboard—my husband used to tease me about that, years ago, because I’d put things in the strangest places when I was distracted.\n\nBut feeling more like myself… I’m not sure I’d recognize it right now. Maybe little moments, maybe not. I haven’t been watching it by the clock.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you’re awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down. I just… I don’t know that I can say “majority of the time.” Some mornings feel very dim, and then I’ll get distracted by some little thing—like whether I paid the water bill, or I’ll notice dust on the lampshade and think I really ought to clean that, though I don’t always do it.\n\nSo I suppose there’s low feelings, yes, but whether it’s most of the time I’m awake… I haven’t been keeping track like that. It all sort of runs together lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the time you're awake, even if it's not the whole day?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked it a few ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult. I just can’t quite tell. The days don’t feel divided up neatly anymore—morning, afternoon, evening, they sort of blur.\n\nI can say I’ve been low, yes, but “most of the time I’m awake”… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just sitting there staring at nothing, and then I’ll suddenly remember I meant to rinse out a cup or move the newspaper, and for a minute I’m thinking about that instead. So I don’t know how to count it, really.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for a good part of the day, even if it's not all day long?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. A good part of the day, yes maybe, but then I think—what counts as a good part? Yesterday I sat by the window for quite a while, and the neighbor’s cat was out there, the orange one, and I remembered we had a cat when I was a girl who used to sleep in the laundry basket.\n\nSo I suppose I do feel down in stretches, but I don’t really know how much of the day it takes up. It isn’t something I’ve measured.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, your low mood is present for a significant portion of the day, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean yes or no. “Significant portion” sounds… official, and I don’t know how to judge that from inside the day.\n\nThere are times I feel quite low, and then I’ll drift off thinking about something else—like whether I left the porch light on, or how my mother used to keep rubber bands in a coffee tin, which is a silly thing to remember in the middle of all this. So yes, it’s there, but whether it’s a significant portion on most days… I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most days, most of the day” exactly. It feels heavy a lot, but then time gets funny, you know? I’ll be sitting in the kitchen and notice the pattern on the tablecloth—my sister had one with little blue flowers, years ago—and then I realize I’ve lost track of whether I was feeling low right then or just… somewhere else.\n\nSo I’m not trying to avoid it. I just don’t know how to measure it that way. It’s there, but I can’t honestly say how much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you are awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down, and I’m not doing a very good job. “Majority of the time I’m awake” is hard for me to say. Some parts of the day I feel very low, and then other parts I’m just sort of… distracted, or I’m watching the kettle, or thinking about where I put my glasses.\n\nI used to keep my glasses on a little chain, you know, my aunt had one with fake pearls on it—funny what comes back to you. But to answer you, I don’t really know if it’s the majority of the day. I haven’t been able to tell it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came. Even when my children sit with me, or when the sun is out on the garden—things that ought to make me feel better—I just feel flat inside.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to notice the light on the leaves, you know, the way it would catch on the tomato cages… my husband used to say I fussed over those plants like they were babies. But now it’s like the part of me that could feel glad, or comforted, has just shut off. I can be there with them, but I don’t really feel it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure how to answer that cleanly. It’s… it comes over me, yes, but “most of the day” feels hard to judge.\n\nSome mornings I’ll sit there with my tea and it feels very low, like everything has gone quiet inside. Then I get pulled into noticing some little thing—the crack in the mug, or I start thinking about the old kitchen curtains we had—and suddenly I don’t know how much time passed or what I was feeling.\n\nSo I don’t want to say yes if that means I’m certain. I just know it’s there a lot, but I can’t really measure it the way you’re asking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Then I get pulled into noticing some little thing—the crack in the mug, or I start thinking about the old kitchen curtains we had—and suddenly I don’t know how much time passed or what I was feeling.\n\nSo I don’t want to say yes if that means I’m certain. I don’t know that I’d say “most of the time.” I mean, I’m old, so tired is such a slippery word, isn’t it? Some days just getting up and making tea feels like a whole production, but then I think, well, maybe that’s just being 81.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I came because I just don’t feel right inside. Even when my children come and sit with me, or the sun is shining on my garden — and I used to notice things like that, you know, the roses especially — I feel flat. Like the part of me that could feel comforted or glad has just shut off. It’s a strange thing to try to explain.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your children?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s it. I used to get such a feeling from my garden, especially when the light came across it in the morning. My children come and sit with me too, and I know I should feel comforted by that—well, I used to. But now it’s like I’m watching it all from behind glass. I feel flat inside, like the part of me that could be glad has just… shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. Down, maybe, but then some days I’m just sort of sitting there and I can’t quite tell what I’m feeling. My daughter says I seem quieter, but she always was one to notice everything—when she was little she’d notice if I moved a vase an inch.\n\nSo… most of the day, nearly every day? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that neatly, dear. Some days I feel low, yes, or… sort of emptied out. But then I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table and start thinking about whether I watered the fern, or whether my son fixed that loose step like he said he would, and I realize I haven’t really noticed my mood in that way.\n\nSo… most of the day, most days? I don’t know. I just know I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a straight yes or no, but I keep losing hold of it. Some days I sit by the window and everything feels heavy, and then I notice the neighbor’s cat in the hydrangeas and I start thinking I ought to tell her, because last summer it flattened a whole patch of flowers.\n\nSo… low for most of the day, most days? I’m not sure. I know I haven’t felt like myself, but I don’t know how to measure it that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, your mood is low or depressed for the entire day, or do you have times during the day when you feel more like yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, doctor. “Entire day” sounds so… exact. There are parts of the day where I’m just quiet, or heavy, I suppose, and then I’ll get up to make tea and remember I left the sugar tin in the wrong cupboard—my husband used to tease me about that, years ago, because I’d put things in the strangest places when I was distracted.\n\nBut feeling more like myself… I’m not sure I’d recognize it right now. Maybe little moments, maybe not. I haven’t been watching it by the clock.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you’re awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down. I just… I don’t know that I can say “majority of the time.” Some mornings feel very dim, and then I’ll get distracted by some little thing—like whether I paid the water bill, or I’ll notice dust on the lampshade and think I really ought to clean that, though I don’t always do it.\n\nSo I suppose there’s low feelings, yes, but whether it’s most of the time I’m awake… I haven’t been keeping track like that. It all sort of runs together lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the time you're awake, even if it's not the whole day?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked it a few ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult. I just can’t quite tell. The days don’t feel divided up neatly anymore—morning, afternoon, evening, they sort of blur.\n\nI can say I’ve been low, yes, but “most of the time I’m awake”… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just sitting there staring at nothing, and then I’ll suddenly remember I meant to rinse out a cup or move the newspaper, and for a minute I’m thinking about that instead. So I don’t know how to count it, really.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for a good part of the day, even if it's not all day long?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. A good part of the day, yes maybe, but then I think—what counts as a good part? Yesterday I sat by the window for quite a while, and the neighbor’s cat was out there, the orange one, and I remembered we had a cat when I was a girl who used to sleep in the laundry basket.\n\nSo I suppose I do feel down in stretches, but I don’t really know how much of the day it takes up. It isn’t something I’ve measured.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, your low mood is present for a significant portion of the day, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean yes or no. “Significant portion” sounds… official, and I don’t know how to judge that from inside the day.\n\nThere are times I feel quite low, and then I’ll drift off thinking about something else—like whether I left the porch light on, or how my mother used to keep rubber bands in a coffee tin, which is a silly thing to remember in the middle of all this. So yes, it’s there, but whether it’s a significant portion on most days… I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most days, most of the day” exactly. It feels heavy a lot, but then time gets funny, you know? I’ll be sitting in the kitchen and notice the pattern on the tablecloth—my sister had one with little blue flowers, years ago—and then I realize I’ve lost track of whether I was feeling low right then or just… somewhere else.\n\nSo I’m not trying to avoid it. I just don’t know how to measure it that way. It’s there, but I can’t honestly say how much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you are awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down, and I’m not doing a very good job. “Majority of the time I’m awake” is hard for me to say. Some parts of the day I feel very low, and then other parts I’m just sort of… distracted, or I’m watching the kettle, or thinking about where I put my glasses.\n\nI used to keep my glasses on a little chain, you know, my aunt had one with fake pearls on it—funny what comes back to you. But to answer you, I don’t really know if it’s the majority of the day. I haven’t been able to tell it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came. Even when my children sit with me, or when the sun is out on the garden—things that ought to make me feel better—I just feel flat inside.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to notice the light on the leaves, you know, the way it would catch on the tomato cages… my husband used to say I fussed over those plants like they were babies. But now it’s like the part of me that could feel glad, or comforted, has just shut off. I can be there with them, but I don’t really feel it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure how to answer that cleanly. It’s… it comes over me, yes, but “most of the day” feels hard to judge.\n\nSome mornings I’ll sit there with my tea and it feels very low, like everything has gone quiet inside. Then I get pulled into noticing some little thing—the crack in the mug, or I start thinking about the old kitchen curtains we had—and suddenly I don’t know how much time passed or what I was feeling.\n\nSo I don’t want to say yes if that means I’m certain. I just know it’s there a lot, but I can’t really measure it the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy most of the time, even when you haven't done much during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “most of the time.” I mean, I’m old, so tired is such a slippery word, isn’t it? Some days just getting up and making tea feels like a whole production, but then I think, well, maybe that’s just being 81.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping track. My mother used to say if you can still put your shoes on, you’re doing all right—though she had those stiff little black shoes, I don’t know how she ever found them comfortable. It’s hard for me to separate ordinary tired from anything else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I just… I don’t know that I can say “majority of the time.” Some mornings feel very dim, and then I’ll get distracted by some little thing—like whether I paid the water bill, or I’ll notice dust on the lampshade and think I really ought to clean that, though I don’t always do it.\n\nSo I suppose there’s low feelings, yes, but whether it’s most of the time I’m awake… I haven’t been keeping track like that. It all sort of runs together lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the time you're awake, even if it's not the whole day?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked it a few ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult. I just can’t quite tell. The days don’t feel divided up neatly anymore—morning, afternoon, evening, they sort of blur.\n\nI can say I’ve been low, yes, but “most of the time I’m awake”… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just sitting there staring at nothing, and then I’ll suddenly remember I meant to rinse out a cup or move the newspaper, and for a minute I’m thinking about that instead. So I don’t know how to count it, really.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for a good part of the day, even if it's not all day long?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. A good part of the day, yes maybe, but then I think—what counts as a good part? Yesterday I sat by the window for quite a while, and the neighbor’s cat was out there, the orange one, and I remembered we had a cat when I was a girl who used to sleep in the laundry basket.\n\nSo I suppose I do feel down in stretches, but I don’t really know how much of the day it takes up. It isn’t something I’ve measured.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, your low mood is present for a significant portion of the day, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean yes or no. “Significant portion” sounds… official, and I don’t know how to judge that from inside the day.\n\nThere are times I feel quite low, and then I’ll drift off thinking about something else—like whether I left the porch light on, or how my mother used to keep rubber bands in a coffee tin, which is a silly thing to remember in the middle of all this. So yes, it’s there, but whether it’s a significant portion on most days… I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most days, most of the day” exactly. It feels heavy a lot, but then time gets funny, you know? I’ll be sitting in the kitchen and notice the pattern on the tablecloth—my sister had one with little blue flowers, years ago—and then I realize I’ve lost track of whether I was feeling low right then or just… somewhere else.\n\nSo I’m not trying to avoid it. I just don’t know how to measure it that way. It’s there, but I can’t honestly say how much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you are awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down, and I’m not doing a very good job. “Majority of the time I’m awake” is hard for me to say. Some parts of the day I feel very low, and then other parts I’m just sort of… distracted, or I’m watching the kettle, or thinking about where I put my glasses.\n\nI used to keep my glasses on a little chain, you know, my aunt had one with fake pearls on it—funny what comes back to you. But to answer you, I don’t really know if it’s the majority of the day. I haven’t been able to tell it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came. Even when my children sit with me, or when the sun is out on the garden—things that ought to make me feel better—I just feel flat inside.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to notice the light on the leaves, you know, the way it would catch on the tomato cages… my husband used to say I fussed over those plants like they were babies. But now it’s like the part of me that could feel glad, or comforted, has just shut off. I can be there with them, but I don’t really feel it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure how to answer that cleanly. It’s… it comes over me, yes, but “most of the day” feels hard to judge.\n\nSome mornings I’ll sit there with my tea and it feels very low, like everything has gone quiet inside. Then I get pulled into noticing some little thing—the crack in the mug, or I start thinking about the old kitchen curtains we had—and suddenly I don’t know how much time passed or what I was feeling.\n\nSo I don’t want to say yes if that means I’m certain. I just know it’s there a lot, but I can’t really measure it the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy most of the time, even when you haven't done much during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “most of the time.” I mean, I’m old, so tired is such a slippery word, isn’t it? Some days just getting up and making tea feels like a whole production, but then I think, well, maybe that’s just being 81.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping track. My mother used to say if you can still put your shoes on, you’re doing all right—though she had those stiff little black shoes, I don’t know how she ever found them comfortable. But… no, I’m not sure I’ve noticed it in the way you mean. It’s hard for me to separate ordinary tired from anything else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I… I’m not really sure. A “full night’s rest” is a funny thing to measure, isn’t it? I can be in bed all night and still not know whether it counts as resting.

And on quiet days, when I haven’t done much, sometimes I feel worn out, but then I think—well, maybe that’s just my age, or maybe it’s the weather. My father used to swear he could feel rain coming in his knees, and we all laughed at him, but now I understand that sort of thing more than I used to.

So… I don’t know that I can say it clearly. I haven’t really noticed it in a way I could be certain about.

It feels heavy a lot, but then time gets funny, you know? I haven’t been watching it by the clock.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you’re awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down. I just… I don’t know that I can say “majority of the time.” Some mornings feel very dim, and then I’ll get distracted by some little thing—like whether I paid the water bill, or I’ll notice dust on the lampshade and think I really ought to clean that, though I don’t always do it.\n\nSo I suppose there’s low feelings, yes, but whether it’s most of the time I’m awake… I haven’t been keeping track like that. It all sort of runs together lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the time you're awake, even if it's not the whole day?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked it a few ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult. I just can’t quite tell. The days don’t feel divided up neatly anymore—morning, afternoon, evening, they sort of blur.\n\nI can say I’ve been low, yes, but “most of the time I’m awake”… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just sitting there staring at nothing, and then I’ll suddenly remember I meant to rinse out a cup or move the newspaper, and for a minute I’m thinking about that instead. So I don’t know how to count it, really.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for a good part of the day, even if it's not all day long?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. A good part of the day, yes maybe, but then I think—what counts as a good part? Yesterday I sat by the window for quite a while, and the neighbor’s cat was out there, the orange one, and I remembered we had a cat when I was a girl who used to sleep in the laundry basket.\n\nSo I suppose I do feel down in stretches, but I don’t really know how much of the day it takes up. It isn’t something I’ve measured.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, your low mood is present for a significant portion of the day, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean yes or no. “Significant portion” sounds… official, and I don’t know how to judge that from inside the day.\n\nThere are times I feel quite low, and then I’ll drift off thinking about something else—like whether I left the porch light on, or how my mother used to keep rubber bands in a coffee tin, which is a silly thing to remember in the middle of all this. So yes, it’s there, but whether it’s a significant portion on most days… I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most days, most of the day” exactly. It feels heavy a lot, but then time gets funny, you know? I’ll be sitting in the kitchen and notice the pattern on the tablecloth—my sister had one with little blue flowers, years ago—and then I realize I’ve lost track of whether I was feeling low right then or just… somewhere else.\n\nSo I’m not trying to avoid it. I just don’t know how to measure it that way. It’s there, but I can’t honestly say how much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you are awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down, and I’m not doing a very good job. “Majority of the time I’m awake” is hard for me to say. Some parts of the day I feel very low, and then other parts I’m just sort of… distracted, or I’m watching the kettle, or thinking about where I put my glasses.\n\nI used to keep my glasses on a little chain, you know, my aunt had one with fake pearls on it—funny what comes back to you. But to answer you, I don’t really know if it’s the majority of the day. I haven’t been able to tell it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came. Even when my children sit with me, or when the sun is out on the garden—things that ought to make me feel better—I just feel flat inside.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to notice the light on the leaves, you know, the way it would catch on the tomato cages… my husband used to say I fussed over those plants like they were babies. But now it’s like the part of me that could feel glad, or comforted, has just shut off. I can be there with them, but I don’t really feel it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure how to answer that cleanly. It’s… it comes over me, yes, but “most of the day” feels hard to judge.\n\nSome mornings I’ll sit there with my tea and it feels very low, like everything has gone quiet inside. Then I get pulled into noticing some little thing—the crack in the mug, or I start thinking about the old kitchen curtains we had—and suddenly I don’t know how much time passed or what I was feeling.\n\nSo I don’t want to say yes if that means I’m certain. I just know it’s there a lot, but I can’t really measure it the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy most of the time, even when you haven't done much during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “most of the time.” I mean, I’m old, so tired is such a slippery word, isn’t it? Some days just getting up and making tea feels like a whole production, but then I think, well, maybe that’s just being 81.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping track. My mother used to say if you can still put your shoes on, you’re doing all right—though she had those stiff little black shoes, I don’t know how she ever found them comfortable. But… no, I’m not sure I’ve noticed it in the way you mean. It’s hard for me to separate ordinary tired from anything else.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel tired or lacking in energy even after a full night’s rest or when you haven’t done much during the day?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure. A “full night’s rest” is a funny thing to measure, isn’t it? I can be in bed all night and still not know whether it counts as resting.\n\nAnd on quiet days, when I haven’t done much, sometimes I feel worn out, but then I think—well, maybe that’s just my age, or maybe it’s the weather. My father used to swear he could feel rain coming in his knees, and we all laughed at him, but now I understand that sort of thing more than I used to.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I can say it clearly. I haven’t really noticed it in a way I could be certain about.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in just about everything, even things you used to enjoy, like tending your garden or being with your children?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s why I came, really. Even when my children are sitting right there with me, trying to be kind, or when the sun is on the garden in that lovely way it used to be—on the roses, you know, though I never did get them to grow as well as my neighbor did—I just feel… flat inside.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know those things should comfort me. I do know. But it’s like the part of me that could feel glad about them has shut off somehow.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

A “full night’s rest” is a funny thing to measure, isn’t it? I can be in bed all night and still not know whether it counts as resting.\n\nAnd on quiet days, when I haven’t done much, sometimes I feel worn out, but then I think—well, maybe that’s just my age, or maybe it’s the weather. My father used to swear he could feel rain coming in his knees, and we all laughed at him, but now I understand that sort of thing more than I used to.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I can say it clearly. I haven’t really noticed it in a way I could be certain about.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in just about everything, even things you used to enjoy, like tending your garden or being with your children?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s why I came, really. Even when my children are sitting right there with me, trying to be kind, or when the sun is on the garden in that lovely way it used to be—on the roses, you know, though I never did get them to grow as well as my neighbor did—I just feel… flat inside.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know those things should comfort me. I do know. But it’s like the part of me that could feel glad about them has shut off somehow.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day, even if it’s not perfectly clear to you how long it lasts?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 81 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can say it that neatly, “most days” and “most of the day.” I do feel low, yes, but then I start wondering when it begins and when it ends, and I lose track of it.

Some mornings I sit there with my cup of tea and it feels like everything is heavy, but then the post comes, or one of the children rings, and I’m distracted for a bit. My husband used to say I could make a cup of tea last an hour because I’d forget it on the table—well, that’s neither here nor there.

So… I suppose I don’t really know how to measure it. I know I haven’t felt right, but I can’t honestly tell you it’s most of the day.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I came because I just don’t feel right inside. Even when my children come and sit with me, or the sun is shining on my garden — and I used to notice things like that, you know, the roses especially — I feel flat. Like the part of me that could feel comforted or glad has just shut off. It’s a strange thing to try to explain.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your children?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s it. I used to get such a feeling from my garden, especially when the light came across it in the morning. My children come and sit with me too, and I know I should feel comforted by that—well, I used to. But now it’s like I’m watching it all from behind glass. I feel flat inside, like the part of me that could be glad has just… shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. Down, maybe, but then some days I’m just sort of sitting there and I can’t quite tell what I’m feeling. My daughter says I seem quieter, but she always was one to notice everything—when she was little she’d notice if I moved a vase an inch.\n\nSo… most of the day, nearly every day? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that neatly, dear. Some days I feel low, yes, or… sort of emptied out. But then I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table and start thinking about whether I watered the fern, or whether my son fixed that loose step like he said he would, and I realize I haven’t really noticed my mood in that way.\n\nSo… most of the day, most days? I don’t know. I just know I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a straight yes or no, but I keep losing hold of it. Some days I sit by the window and everything feels heavy, and then I notice the neighbor’s cat in the hydrangeas and I start thinking I ought to tell her, because last summer it flattened a whole patch of flowers.\n\nSo… low for most of the day, most days? I’m not sure. I know I haven’t felt like myself, but I don’t know how to measure it that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, your mood is low or depressed for the entire day, or do you have times during the day when you feel more like yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, doctor. “Entire day” sounds so… exact. There are parts of the day where I’m just quiet, or heavy, I suppose, and then I’ll get up to make tea and remember I left the sugar tin in the wrong cupboard—my husband used to tease me about that, years ago, because I’d put things in the strangest places when I was distracted.\n\nBut feeling more like myself… I’m not sure I’d recognize it right now. Maybe little moments, maybe not. I haven’t been watching it by the clock.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you’re awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down. I just… I don’t know that I can say “majority of the time.” Some mornings feel very dim, and then I’ll get distracted by some little thing—like whether I paid the water bill, or I’ll notice dust on the lampshade and think I really ought to clean that, though I don’t always do it.\n\nSo I suppose there’s low feelings, yes, but whether it’s most of the time I’m awake… I haven’t been keeping track like that. It all sort of runs together lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the time you're awake, even if it's not the whole day?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked it a few ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult. I just can’t quite tell. The days don’t feel divided up neatly anymore—morning, afternoon, evening, they sort of blur.\n\nI can say I’ve been low, yes, but “most of the time I’m awake”… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just sitting there staring at nothing, and then I’ll suddenly remember I meant to rinse out a cup or move the newspaper, and for a minute I’m thinking about that instead. So I don’t know how to count it, really.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for a good part of the day, even if it's not all day long?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. A good part of the day, yes maybe, but then I think—what counts as a good part? Yesterday I sat by the window for quite a while, and the neighbor’s cat was out there, the orange one, and I remembered we had a cat when I was a girl who used to sleep in the laundry basket.\n\nSo I suppose I do feel down in stretches, but I don’t really know how much of the day it takes up. It isn’t something I’ve measured.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, your low mood is present for a significant portion of the day, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean yes or no. “Significant portion” sounds… official, and I don’t know how to judge that from inside the day.\n\nThere are times I feel quite low, and then I’ll drift off thinking about something else—like whether I left the porch light on, or how my mother used to keep rubber bands in a coffee tin, which is a silly thing to remember in the middle of all this. So yes, it’s there, but whether it’s a significant portion on most days… I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most days, most of the day” exactly. It feels heavy a lot, but then time gets funny, you know? I’ll be sitting in the kitchen and notice the pattern on the tablecloth—my sister had one with little blue flowers, years ago—and then I realize I’ve lost track of whether I was feeling low right then or just… somewhere else.\n\nSo I’m not trying to avoid it. I just don’t know how to measure it that way. It’s there, but I can’t honestly say how much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you are awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down, and I’m not doing a very good job. “Majority of the time I’m awake” is hard for me to say. Some parts of the day I feel very low, and then other parts I’m just sort of… distracted, or I’m watching the kettle, or thinking about where I put my glasses.\n\nI used to keep my glasses on a little chain, you know, my aunt had one with fake pearls on it—funny what comes back to you. But to answer you, I don’t really know if it’s the majority of the day. I haven’t been able to tell it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came. Even when my children sit with me, or when the sun is out on the garden—things that ought to make me feel better—I just feel flat inside.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to notice the light on the leaves, you know, the way it would catch on the tomato cages… my husband used to say I fussed over those plants like they were babies. But now it’s like the part of me that could feel glad, or comforted, has just shut off. I can be there with them, but I don’t really feel it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure how to answer that cleanly. It’s… it comes over me, yes, but “most of the day” feels hard to judge.\n\nSome mornings I’ll sit there with my tea and it feels very low, like everything has gone quiet inside. Then I get pulled into noticing some little thing—the crack in the mug, or I start thinking about the old kitchen curtains we had—and suddenly I don’t know how much time passed or what I was feeling.\n\nSo I don’t want to say yes if that means I’m certain. I just know it’s there a lot, but I can’t really measure it the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy most of the time, even when you haven't done much during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “most of the time.” I mean, I’m old, so tired is such a slippery word, isn’t it? Some days just getting up and making tea feels like a whole production, but then I think, well, maybe that’s just being 81.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping track. My mother used to say if you can still put your shoes on, you’re doing all right—though she had those stiff little black shoes, I don’t know how she ever found them comfortable. But… no, I’m not sure I’ve noticed it in the way you mean. It’s hard for me to separate ordinary tired from anything else.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel tired or lacking in energy even after a full night’s rest or when you haven’t done much during the day?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure. A “full night’s rest” is a funny thing to measure, isn’t it? I can be in bed all night and still not know whether it counts as resting.\n\nAnd on quiet days, when I haven’t done much, sometimes I feel worn out, but then I think—well, maybe that’s just my age, or maybe it’s the weather. My father used to swear he could feel rain coming in his knees, and we all laughed at him, but now I understand that sort of thing more than I used to.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I can say it clearly. I haven’t really noticed it in a way I could be certain about.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in just about everything, even things you used to enjoy, like tending your garden or being with your children?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s why I came, really. Even when my children are sitting right there with me, trying to be kind, or when the sun is on the garden in that lovely way it used to be—on the roses, you know, though I never did get them to grow as well as my neighbor did—I just feel… flat inside.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know those things should comfort me. I do know. My husband used to say I could make a cup of tea last an hour because I’d forget it on the table—well, that’s neither here nor there.\n\nSo… I suppose I don’t really know how to measure it. I know you asked that already, and I’m sorry—I’m not trying to be difficult. It’s just hard for me to pin down.

I do feel low sometimes, yes, but “most of the day”… I don’t know. I lose track of the hours. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. What brings you here today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I came because I just don’t feel right inside. Even when my children come and sit with me, or the sun is shining on my garden — and I used to notice things like that, you know, the roses especially — I feel flat. Like the part of me that could feel comforted or glad has just shut off. It’s a strange thing to try to explain.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your children?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s it. I used to get such a feeling from my garden, especially when the light came across it in the morning. My children come and sit with me too, and I know I should feel comforted by that—well, I used to. But now it’s like I’m watching it all from behind glass. I feel flat inside, like the part of me that could be glad has just… shut off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. Down, maybe, but then some days I’m just sort of sitting there and I can’t quite tell what I’m feeling. My daughter says I seem quieter, but she always was one to notice everything—when she was little she’d notice if I moved a vase an inch.\n\nSo… most of the day, nearly every day? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that neatly, dear. Some days I feel low, yes, or… sort of emptied out. But then I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table and start thinking about whether I watered the fern, or whether my son fixed that loose step like he said he would, and I realize I haven’t really noticed my mood in that way.\n\nSo… most of the day, most days? I don’t know. I just know I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a straight yes or no, but I keep losing hold of it. Some days I sit by the window and everything feels heavy, and then I notice the neighbor’s cat in the hydrangeas and I start thinking I ought to tell her, because last summer it flattened a whole patch of flowers.\n\nSo… low for most of the day, most days? I’m not sure. I know I haven’t felt like myself, but I don’t know how to measure it that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, your mood is low or depressed for the entire day, or do you have times during the day when you feel more like yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, doctor. “Entire day” sounds so… exact. There are parts of the day where I’m just quiet, or heavy, I suppose, and then I’ll get up to make tea and remember I left the sugar tin in the wrong cupboard—my husband used to tease me about that, years ago, because I’d put things in the strangest places when I was distracted.\n\nBut feeling more like myself… I’m not sure I’d recognize it right now. Maybe little moments, maybe not. I haven’t been watching it by the clock.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you’re awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down. I just… I don’t know that I can say “majority of the time.” Some mornings feel very dim, and then I’ll get distracted by some little thing—like whether I paid the water bill, or I’ll notice dust on the lampshade and think I really ought to clean that, though I don’t always do it.\n\nSo I suppose there’s low feelings, yes, but whether it’s most of the time I’m awake… I haven’t been keeping track like that. It all sort of runs together lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the time you're awake, even if it's not the whole day?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked it a few ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult. I just can’t quite tell. The days don’t feel divided up neatly anymore—morning, afternoon, evening, they sort of blur.\n\nI can say I’ve been low, yes, but “most of the time I’m awake”… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just sitting there staring at nothing, and then I’ll suddenly remember I meant to rinse out a cup or move the newspaper, and for a minute I’m thinking about that instead. So I don’t know how to count it, really.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for a good part of the day, even if it's not all day long?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. A good part of the day, yes maybe, but then I think—what counts as a good part? Yesterday I sat by the window for quite a while, and the neighbor’s cat was out there, the orange one, and I remembered we had a cat when I was a girl who used to sleep in the laundry basket.\n\nSo I suppose I do feel down in stretches, but I don’t really know how much of the day it takes up. It isn’t something I’ve measured.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, your low mood is present for a significant portion of the day, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean yes or no. “Significant portion” sounds… official, and I don’t know how to judge that from inside the day.\n\nThere are times I feel quite low, and then I’ll drift off thinking about something else—like whether I left the porch light on, or how my mother used to keep rubber bands in a coffee tin, which is a silly thing to remember in the middle of all this. So yes, it’s there, but whether it’s a significant portion on most days… I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most days, most of the day” exactly. It feels heavy a lot, but then time gets funny, you know? I’ll be sitting in the kitchen and notice the pattern on the tablecloth—my sister had one with little blue flowers, years ago—and then I realize I’ve lost track of whether I was feeling low right then or just… somewhere else.\n\nSo I’m not trying to avoid it. I just don’t know how to measure it that way. It’s there, but I can’t honestly say how much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you are awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down, and I’m not doing a very good job. “Majority of the time I’m awake” is hard for me to say. Some parts of the day I feel very low, and then other parts I’m just sort of… distracted, or I’m watching the kettle, or thinking about where I put my glasses.\n\nI used to keep my glasses on a little chain, you know, my aunt had one with fake pearls on it—funny what comes back to you. But to answer you, I don’t really know if it’s the majority of the day. I haven’t been able to tell it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came. Even when my children sit with me, or when the sun is out on the garden—things that ought to make me feel better—I just feel flat inside.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to notice the light on the leaves, you know, the way it would catch on the tomato cages… my husband used to say I fussed over those plants like they were babies. But now it’s like the part of me that could feel glad, or comforted, has just shut off. I can be there with them, but I don’t really feel it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure how to answer that cleanly. But… no, I’m not sure I’ve noticed it in the way you mean. It’s hard for me to separate ordinary tired from anything else.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel tired or lacking in energy even after a full night’s rest or when you haven’t done much during the day?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure.
