My daughter says I seem quieter, but she always was one to notice everything—when she was little she’d notice if I moved a vase an inch.\n\nSo… most of the day, nearly every day? I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that neatly, dear. Some days I feel low, yes, or… sort of emptied out. But then I’ll be sitting at the kitchen table and start thinking about whether I watered the fern, or whether my son fixed that loose step like he said he would, and I realize I haven’t really noticed my mood in that way.\n\nSo… most of the day, most days? I don’t know. I just know I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a straight yes or no, but I keep losing hold of it. Some days I sit by the window and everything feels heavy, and then I notice the neighbor’s cat in the hydrangeas and I start thinking I ought to tell her, because last summer it flattened a whole patch of flowers.\n\nSo… low for most of the day, most days? I’m not sure. I know I haven’t felt like myself, but I don’t know how to measure it that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, your mood is low or depressed for the entire day, or do you have times during the day when you feel more like yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, doctor. “Entire day” sounds so… exact. There are parts of the day where I’m just quiet, or heavy, I suppose, and then I’ll get up to make tea and remember I left the sugar tin in the wrong cupboard—my husband used to tease me about that, years ago, because I’d put things in the strangest places when I was distracted.\n\nBut feeling more like myself… I’m not sure I’d recognize it right now. Maybe little moments, maybe not. I haven’t been watching it by the clock.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you’re awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down. I just… I don’t know that I can say “majority of the time.” Some mornings feel very dim, and then I’ll get distracted by some little thing—like whether I paid the water bill, or I’ll notice dust on the lampshade and think I really ought to clean that, though I don’t always do it.\n\nSo I suppose there’s low feelings, yes, but whether it’s most of the time I’m awake… I haven’t been keeping track like that. It all sort of runs together lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the time you're awake, even if it's not the whole day?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked it a few ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult. I just can’t quite tell. The days don’t feel divided up neatly anymore—morning, afternoon, evening, they sort of blur.\n\nI can say I’ve been low, yes, but “most of the time I’m awake”… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just sitting there staring at nothing, and then I’ll suddenly remember I meant to rinse out a cup or move the newspaper, and for a minute I’m thinking about that instead. So I don’t know how to count it, really.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for a good part of the day, even if it's not all day long?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. A good part of the day, yes maybe, but then I think—what counts as a good part? Yesterday I sat by the window for quite a while, and the neighbor’s cat was out there, the orange one, and I remembered we had a cat when I was a girl who used to sleep in the laundry basket.\n\nSo I suppose I do feel down in stretches, but I don’t really know how much of the day it takes up. It isn’t something I’ve measured.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, your low mood is present for a significant portion of the day, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean yes or no. “Significant portion” sounds… official, and I don’t know how to judge that from inside the day.\n\nThere are times I feel quite low, and then I’ll drift off thinking about something else—like whether I left the porch light on, or how my mother used to keep rubber bands in a coffee tin, which is a silly thing to remember in the middle of all this. So yes, it’s there, but whether it’s a significant portion on most days… I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most days, most of the day” exactly. It feels heavy a lot, but then time gets funny, you know? I’ll be sitting in the kitchen and notice the pattern on the tablecloth—my sister had one with little blue flowers, years ago—and then I realize I’ve lost track of whether I was feeling low right then or just… somewhere else.\n\nSo I’m not trying to avoid it. I just don’t know how to measure it that way. It’s there, but I can’t honestly say how much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you are awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down, and I’m not doing a very good job. “Majority of the time I’m awake” is hard for me to say. Some parts of the day I feel very low, and then other parts I’m just sort of… distracted, or I’m watching the kettle, or thinking about where I put my glasses.\n\nI used to keep my glasses on a little chain, you know, my aunt had one with fake pearls on it—funny what comes back to you. But to answer you, I don’t really know if it’s the majority of the day. I haven’t been able to tell it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came. Even when my children sit with me, or when the sun is out on the garden—things that ought to make me feel better—I just feel flat inside.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to notice the light on the leaves, you know, the way it would catch on the tomato cages… my husband used to say I fussed over those plants like they were babies. But now it’s like the part of me that could feel glad, or comforted, has just shut off. I can be there with them, but I don’t really feel it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure how to answer that cleanly. It’s… it comes over me, yes, but “most of the day” feels hard to judge.\n\nSome mornings I’ll sit there with my tea and it feels very low, like everything has gone quiet inside. Then I get pulled into noticing some little thing—the crack in the mug, or I start thinking about the old kitchen curtains we had—and suddenly I don’t know how much time passed or what I was feeling.\n\nSo I don’t want to say yes if that means I’m certain. I just know it’s there a lot, but I can’t really measure it the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy most of the time, even when you haven't done much during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “most of the time.” I mean, I’m old, so tired is such a slippery word, isn’t it? Some days just getting up and making tea feels like a whole production, but then I think, well, maybe that’s just being 81.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping track. My mother used to say if you can still put your shoes on, you’re doing all right—though she had those stiff little black shoes, I don’t know how she ever found them comfortable. But… no, I’m not sure I’ve noticed it in the way you mean. It’s hard for me to separate ordinary tired from anything else.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel tired or lacking in energy even after a full night’s rest or when you haven’t done much during the day?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure. A “full night’s rest” is a funny thing to measure, isn’t it? I can be in bed all night and still not know whether it counts as resting.\n\nAnd on quiet days, when I haven’t done much, sometimes I feel worn out, but then I think—well, maybe that’s just my age, or maybe it’s the weather. My father used to swear he could feel rain coming in his knees, and we all laughed at him, but now I understand that sort of thing more than I used to.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I can say it clearly. I haven’t really noticed it in a way I could be certain about.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in just about everything, even things you used to enjoy, like tending your garden or being with your children?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s why I came, really. Even when my children are sitting right there with me, trying to be kind, or when the sun is on the garden in that lovely way it used to be—on the roses, you know, though I never did get them to grow as well as my neighbor did—I just feel… flat inside.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know those things should comfort me. I do know. But it’s like the part of me that could feel glad about them has shut off somehow.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day, even if it’s not perfectly clear to you how long it lasts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that neatly, “most days” and “most of the day.” I do feel low, yes, but then I start wondering when it begins and when it ends, and I lose track of it.\n\nSome mornings I sit there with my cup of tea and it feels like everything is heavy, but then the post comes, or one of the children rings, and I’m distracted for a bit. It’s just hard for me to pin down.\n\nI do feel low sometimes, yes, but “most of the day”… I don’t know. I lose track of the hours. I might be sitting in the chair and then suddenly it’s lunchtime, or I’ll look out the window at the garden and start thinking about whether the hydrangeas should’ve been cut back earlier—my neighbor always had strong opinions about that.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say it that clearly. I’m trying to answer you properly, but I still don’t quite know. “More often than not” sounds simple, doesn’t it, but when I think back over a day it sort of breaks into pieces.

There are times I feel very low, yes… and then something ordinary happens, like the kettle boiling over or I notice dust on the sideboard, and I’m off thinking about that. My mother used to say dust was proof people lived in a house, though she still polished everything twice a week.

So I suppose I can say I haven’t felt myself. But whether it’s more of the day than not… I’m not certain. I don’t want to say yes if I’m only guessing.

I just… I don’t know that I can say “majority of the time.” Some mornings feel very dim, and then I’ll get distracted by some little thing—like whether I paid the water bill, or I’ll notice dust on the lampshade and think I really ought to clean that, though I don’t always do it.\n\nSo I suppose there’s low feelings, yes, but whether it’s most of the time I’m awake… I haven’t been keeping track like that. It all sort of runs together lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the time you're awake, even if it's not the whole day?\n\nPatient: I know you’ve asked it a few ways, and I’m not trying to be difficult. I just can’t quite tell. The days don’t feel divided up neatly anymore—morning, afternoon, evening, they sort of blur.\n\nI can say I’ve been low, yes, but “most of the time I’m awake”… I don’t know. Sometimes I’m just sitting there staring at nothing, and then I’ll suddenly remember I meant to rinse out a cup or move the newspaper, and for a minute I’m thinking about that instead. So I don’t know how to count it, really.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for a good part of the day, even if it's not all day long?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m not sure how to answer that neatly. A good part of the day, yes maybe, but then I think—what counts as a good part? Yesterday I sat by the window for quite a while, and the neighbor’s cat was out there, the orange one, and I remembered we had a cat when I was a girl who used to sleep in the laundry basket.\n\nSo I suppose I do feel down in stretches, but I don’t really know how much of the day it takes up. It isn’t something I’ve measured.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, your low mood is present for a significant portion of the day, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean yes or no. “Significant portion” sounds… official, and I don’t know how to judge that from inside the day.\n\nThere are times I feel quite low, and then I’ll drift off thinking about something else—like whether I left the porch light on, or how my mother used to keep rubber bands in a coffee tin, which is a silly thing to remember in the middle of all this. So yes, it’s there, but whether it’s a significant portion on most days… I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, for most of the day, you feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say “most days, most of the day” exactly. It feels heavy a lot, but then time gets funny, you know? I’ll be sitting in the kitchen and notice the pattern on the tablecloth—my sister had one with little blue flowers, years ago—and then I realize I’ve lost track of whether I was feeling low right then or just… somewhere else.\n\nSo I’m not trying to avoid it. I just don’t know how to measure it that way. It’s there, but I can’t honestly say how much of the day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if, on most days, you feel down or depressed for the majority of the time you are awake, even if it’s not the entire day?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to pin it down, and I’m not doing a very good job. “Majority of the time I’m awake” is hard for me to say. Some parts of the day I feel very low, and then other parts I’m just sort of… distracted, or I’m watching the kettle, or thinking about where I put my glasses.\n\nI used to keep my glasses on a little chain, you know, my aunt had one with fake pearls on it—funny what comes back to you. But to answer you, I don’t really know if it’s the majority of the day. I haven’t been able to tell it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or spending time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of why I came. Even when my children sit with me, or when the sun is out on the garden—things that ought to make me feel better—I just feel flat inside.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to notice the light on the leaves, you know, the way it would catch on the tomato cages… my husband used to say I fussed over those plants like they were babies. But now it’s like the part of me that could feel glad, or comforted, has just shut off. I can be there with them, but I don’t really feel it the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure how to answer that cleanly. It’s… it comes over me, yes, but “most of the day” feels hard to judge.\n\nSome mornings I’ll sit there with my tea and it feels very low, like everything has gone quiet inside. Then I get pulled into noticing some little thing—the crack in the mug, or I start thinking about the old kitchen curtains we had—and suddenly I don’t know how much time passed or what I was feeling.\n\nSo I don’t want to say yes if that means I’m certain. I just know it’s there a lot, but I can’t really measure it the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling tired or lacking energy most of the time, even when you haven't done much during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “most of the time.” I mean, I’m old, so tired is such a slippery word, isn’t it? Some days just getting up and making tea feels like a whole production, but then I think, well, maybe that’s just being 81.\n\nI haven’t really been keeping track. My mother used to say if you can still put your shoes on, you’re doing all right—though she had those stiff little black shoes, I don’t know how she ever found them comfortable. But… no, I’m not sure I’ve noticed it in the way you mean. It’s hard for me to separate ordinary tired from anything else.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel tired or lacking in energy even after a full night’s rest or when you haven’t done much during the day?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure. A “full night’s rest” is a funny thing to measure, isn’t it? I can be in bed all night and still not know whether it counts as resting.\n\nAnd on quiet days, when I haven’t done much, sometimes I feel worn out, but then I think—well, maybe that’s just my age, or maybe it’s the weather. My father used to swear he could feel rain coming in his knees, and we all laughed at him, but now I understand that sort of thing more than I used to.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I can say it clearly. I haven’t really noticed it in a way I could be certain about.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest or pleasure in just about everything, even things you used to enjoy, like tending your garden or being with your children?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s why I came, really. Even when my children are sitting right there with me, trying to be kind, or when the sun is on the garden in that lovely way it used to be—on the roses, you know, though I never did get them to grow as well as my neighbor did—I just feel… flat inside.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know those things should comfort me. I do know. But it’s like the part of me that could feel glad about them has shut off somehow.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day, even if it’s not perfectly clear to you how long it lasts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it that neatly, “most days” and “most of the day.” I do feel low, yes, but then I start wondering when it begins and when it ends, and I lose track of it.\n\nSome mornings I sit there with my cup of tea and it feels like everything is heavy, but then the post comes, or one of the children rings, and I’m distracted for a bit. My husband used to say I could make a cup of tea last an hour because I’d forget it on the table—well, that’s neither here nor there.\n\nSo… I suppose I don’t really know how to measure it. I know I haven’t felt right, but I can’t honestly tell you it’s most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel down or low in mood for most of the day, even if it’s not perfectly clear how long it lasts?\n\nPatient: I know you asked that already, and I’m sorry—I’m not trying to be difficult. It’s just hard for me to pin down.\n\nI do feel low sometimes, yes, but “most of the day”… I don’t know. I lose track of the hours. I might be sitting in the chair and then suddenly it’s lunchtime, or I’ll look out the window at the garden and start thinking about whether the hydrangeas should’ve been cut back earlier—my neighbor always had strong opinions about that.\n\nSo… I can’t honestly say it that clearly. I know something isn’t right, but I don’t know if it’s most days or most of the day.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, overall, you feel down or low in mood more often than not throughout the day, even if it’s not perfectly clear how long it lasts each time?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to answer you properly, but I still don’t quite know. “More often than not” sounds simple, doesn’t it, but when I think back over a day it sort of breaks into pieces.\n\nThere are times I feel very low, yes… and then something ordinary happens, like the kettle boiling over or I notice dust on the sideboard, and I’m off thinking about that. My mother used to say dust was proof people lived in a house, though she still polished everything twice a week.\n\nSo I suppose I can say I haven’t felt myself. But whether it’s more of the day than not… I’m not certain. I don’t want to say yes if I’m only guessing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
